## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:53:39.878Z

A 202 status should count as success since ok covers 200-299, so I'm wondering if the error instead comes from the connection dropping before the response arrives, causing a swallowed transport failure. That makes me suspect the actual restart succeeded but something else in the flow reported failure incorrectly—maybe the restart function itself returned an unexpected value.

Actually, I don't think it's a code-level thrown error from an unavailable control path, since the restart genuinely happened. Nothing between the restart request and the dialog close should throw, so maybe the issue is timing: the restart request might not finish before the underlying process gets killed.

That means the browser's fetch could reject with a raw network error rather than a proper API response, and if the request wrapper reshapes all failures into an object with a generic code, that would explain the mismatch — it'd bypass the transport-failure check and produce the generic error message seen in the report. I want to look at how request.ts handles a fetch rejection to confirm this.

8	  for (const [key, value] of Object.entries(params)) {
9	    if (value != null && value !== "") {
10	      search.set(key, value);
11	    }

15	}

21	  return JSON.parse(bodyText) as unknown;
22	}
23	
24	export function normalizeRuntimeHttpError({
25	  status,
26	  body,
27	  surface,
28	}: {
29	  status: number;
30	  body: unknown;
31	  surface: RuntimeSurface;
32	}): RuntimeApiError {
33	  const payload = body && typeof body === "object" && "error" in body
34	    ? (body as {

37	        message?: unknown;
38	        details?: unknown;
39	        surface?: unknown;
40	      };
41	    }).error
42	    : null;
43	  const isBareServerUnavailable = status === 502 && payload === null;
44	  return {
45	    code: typeof payload?.code === "string"
46	      ? payload.code
47	      : `runtime.http_${status}`,
48	    message: typeof payload?.message === "string"
49	      ? payload.message
50	      : isBareServerUnavailable
51	        ? t("runtime.connection.serverUnavailable")
52	      : `Runtime request failed with HTTP ${status}.`,

56	      : surface,

67	export function isRuntimeTransportFailure(error: unknown) {

69	}

103	    || value instanceof FormData
104	    || value instanceof URLSearchParams
105	    || value instanceof ArrayBuffer
106	    || ArrayBuffer.isView(value)
107	    || (typeof ReadableStream !== "undefined" && value instanceof ReadableStream);
108	}
109	
110	export function createFetchRuntimeRequest({
111	  baseUrl = "",
112	  fetchImpl,
113	  surface,
114	  bearerToken,
115	}: {
116	  baseUrl?: string;
117	  fetchImpl: typeof fetch;
118	  surface: RuntimeSurface;
119	  bearerToken?: string | (() => string | null);
120	}): RuntimeDomainRequest {
121	  const joinUrl = (requestPath: string) => baseUrl
122	    ? `${baseUrl.replace(/\/$/, "")}${requestPath}`
123	    : requestPath;
124	
125	  return async (method, requestPath, options = {}) => {
126	    const headers = new Headers(options.headers);
127	    const token = typeof bearerToken === "function" ? bearerToken() : bearerToken;
128	    if (token) {
129	      headers.set("authorization", `Bearer ${token}`);
130	    }
131	    let body: BodyInit | undefined;
132	    if (options.body !== undefined) {
133	      if (isBodyInit(options.body)) {
134	        body = options.body;
135	      } else {
136	        headers.set("content-type", "application/json");
137	        body = JSON.stringify(options.body);
138	      }
139	    }

178	      parsedBody = await response.blob();

180	      parsedBody = await response.arrayBuffer();

188	    }

193	      };
194	    }
195	    return parsedBody;
196	  };
197	}

1	import fs from "fs/promises";
2	import path from "path";
3	import { resolveRestartControlConfig } from "@/server/restart-control";
4	
5	/**
6	 * A runner cannot restart itself: the process serving the request is the one
7	 * that has to die, so it could never send a response or bring itself back. The
8	 * restart-control server (a separate process, port 3099 by default) owns the
9	 * lifecycle for exactly that reason.
10	 *
11	 * So "Restart server" in the UI proxies to that control server. The browser
12	 * cannot call it directly — it is a different origin and its bearer token lives
13	 * in a 0600 file on disk — so the runner reads the token and forwards the call.
14	 */
15	export type RunnerRestartOutcome =
16	  | { ok: true; pid: number | null; mode: string | null; startedAt: number | null }
17	  | { ok: false; code: "control_unavailable" | "control_unauthorized" | "control_failed"; message: string };
18	
19	const CONTROL_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS = 30_000;
20	
21	async function readRestartToken(repoRoot: string, configuredToken: string | null) {
22	  if (configuredToken?.trim()) {
23	    return configuredToken.trim();
24	  }
25	  try {
26	    const raw = await fs.readFile(path.join(repoRoot, ".omniharness", "remote-restart-token"), "utf8");
27	    return raw.trim() || null;
28	  } catch {
29	    return null;
30	  }
31	}
32	
33	function isConnectionRefused(error: unknown) {
34	  const chain: unknown[] = [error, (error as { cause?: unknown } | null)?.cause];
35	  return chain.some((entry) => {
36	    const code = (entry as { code?: unknown } | null)?.code;
37	    const message = entry instanceof Error ? entry.message : "";
38	    return code === "ECONNREFUSED" || /\bECONNREFUSED\b|\bfetch failed\b/i.test(message);
39	  });
40	}
41	
42	export async function requestRunnerRestart(options: {
43	  repoRoot?: string;
44	  env?: Record<string, string | undefined>;
45	  fetchImpl?: typeof fetch;
46	} = {}): Promise<RunnerRestartOutcome> {
47	  const repoRoot = options.repoRoot ?? process.cwd();
48	  const env = options.env ?? process.env;
49	  const doFetch = options.fetchImpl ?? fetch;
50	  const config = resolveRestartControlConfig(repoRoot, env);
51	  const token = await readRestartToken(repoRoot, config.token ?? null);
52	
53	  if (!token) {
54	    return {
55	      ok: false,
56	      code: "control_unavailable",
57	      message: "The restart control service has no token on this machine. Start it with `pnpm restart:control`.",
58	    };
59	  }
60	
61	  try {
62	    const response = await doFetch(`http://127.0.0.1:${config.port}/restart-current`, {
63	      method: "POST",
64	      headers: {
65	        Authorization: `Bearer ${token}`,
66	        Accept: "application/json",
67	      },

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:54:04.372Z

I'm realizing that the restart happens while the runner's own request is still in-flight - if the control server kills the runner process mid-fetch, the runner dies before sending any response, so the client just sees a dropped connection rather than a clean reply.

But that doesn't quite explain the reported case: the restart actually succeeded, yet the user still saw an error. That makes me wonder if the failure came from a different code path entirely - maybe the restart dialog isn't tied to `handleDialogAction` at all, and the generic "server operation failed" message is being triggered elsewhere.

Only a coded rejection can surface this specific error, since it's set from a catch block. Session auth failures seem unlikely since the restart clearly went through, so I'm now looking at whether the control server itself could return a non-2xx status yet still trigger the restart before failing to respond properly - or whether a timeout (say, 30 seconds) could fire if the control server is slow because it's mid-restart.

Actually the more compelling angle is graceful shutdown behavior: if the runner's HTTP server sends a real 503 response while shutting down before starting the new instance, that would produce an actual HTTP status code rather than a transport failure, mapping to a generic error code instead of a network-error message. I want to check how the shutdown logic in the http server actually behaves to confirm this.

I want to trace whether the client dialog actually calls the runtime restart method, since if the runtime connection is undefined that call would just be a silent no-op rather than throwing. I should check the RunnerConnection code around where it throws a generic runner error, since the connection layer might be wrapping the runtime API and rethrowing on disconnect.

381	type RestartCurrentController = Pick<ReturnType<typeof createRestartController>, "restartCurrent">;
382	
383	/**
384	 * Restart the current runner, acknowledging before anything is stopped.
385	 *
386	 * The caller here is the runner itself, and the restart kills it partway
387	 * through. Replying only once the restart had finished wrote that reply to a
388	 * socket whose client died a second earlier, so the browser that asked saw a
389	 * failed request for a restart that had in fact worked. Before anything is
390	 * stopped is the only moment the caller is still alive to hear an answer.
391	 *
392	 * The trade is that a restart failing after the acknowledgement can no longer be
393	 * reported to that caller, so it goes to `onFailure` instead. The kill can also
394	 * still land before the acknowledgement reaches the client, so callers have to
395	 * tolerate losing the connection outright as well.
396	 */
397	export async function restartCurrentWithEarlyAck({
398	  controller,

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:54:42.586Z

The control server acknowledges quickly then restarts, so the runner should get a fast 200 and pass a 202 back to the browser successfully—unless the kill happens before that 202 reaches the client, which the comment already accounts for as a tolerated failure mode. So I'm trying to pinpoint what other gap exists, considering what actually happens on the wire if the runner process gets killed mid-response after it's already started writing the HTTP output.

If the socket gets destroyed after headers are sent, the fetch call resolves but reading the body throws a network error with no error code, so it gets swallowed as a transport failure — that path checks out. So maybe the problematic call isn't the restart request itself but something happening afterward, like the uiManager.close() call inside the same try block, though that seems unlikely to throw. I'm now wondering if the error actually gets set somewhere else entirely — perhaps a connection status watcher reacts to the restart-induced disconnect and sets the dialog error independently, so I want to trace all the callers of setError on the RunnerUiManager.

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:55:23.981Z

There's no api-error logged for the restart endpoint and no runner.restart_failed entry, meaning the runner never got the chance to log a failure — it likely replied 202 and then died almost instantly, since the restart request and completion timestamps are the same second. The kill probably landed within milliseconds of that acknowledgment going out.

Given the early-ack pattern, the browser would most likely see a severed connection resulting in a TypeError that gets classified as a transport failure and silently swallowed — but the user did see an error, so something else must be surfacing it. I'm wondering if there's a proxy in play: a proxy sitting between the browser and runner would return a 502 when the upstream dies, and that maps to a coded runtime error rather than being treated as a transport failure, producing a visible generic error message — and I notice the error-normalization logic already has a special case for a bare 502 meaning "server unavailable," suggesting this exact scenario was anticipated before.

I check whether a dev proxy is actually in use, but since they're running the production build where the runner serves the interface itself, there's no separate origin—so that's probably not it. That makes me consider the service worker angle instead, since this is a PWA with its own bootstrap and lib modules that could be intercepting requests independently.

I'm now leaning toward the idea that a service worker intercepting a failed fetch could synthesize its own response (like a 503 offline fallback), which would again map to a coded error and produce the same generic message users saw. Rather than getting too attached to pinning down the exact mechanism, though, I realize the better fix is more principled: since the restart call is inherently fire-and-forget (the server acks before anything stops, and a dying process can't reliably report back), the dialog should only treat explicit, well-formed restart-control error codes as real failures, and swallow everything else — narrowing the swallow condition from "transport failure only" to "anything except an explicit restart-control error."

That change would also cover 502s from proxies, service worker fallbacks, and aborted requests. I'm considering whether to surface a dedicated error message for those genuine restart-control failures rather than the generic one, but that would mean adding new locale keys across all the locale files, which expands scope beyond what was asked. I want to keep the fix tight — swallow non-explicit failures, and only show a specific message for the real ones — but I'm weighing whether the locale updates are worth it given there's already a separate localization refresh commit in flight.

Before committing to this fix though, I should verify my hypothesis about the service worker actually causing the false error, since if the real culprit is something else — like an exception thrown inside a UI cleanup function — this change wouldn't fix anything. Let me go look at the service worker's fetch handling logic to confirm.

self.addEventListener("fetch", (event) => {
  const { request } = event;
  if (request.method !== "GET") {
    return;
  }

  const url = new URL(request.url);

  // A service worker is scoped to its own runner. Other runners, and all
  // runner API/SSE traffic, must always use the network unchanged.
  if (url.origin !== self.location.origin || isSameOriginApi(url)) {
    return;

      try {

    if (cached) {
      return cached;
    }
    const response = await fetch(request);
    if (response.ok && response.type !== "opaque") {
      const cache = await caches.open(CACHE_NAME);
      await cache.put(url.pathname, response.clone());
    }
    return response;
  })());
});

Another possibility is an API revision mismatch getting converted into a coded error somewhere—worth checking the web runtime API factory for any such wrapper.

  baseUrl?: string;

  bearerToken?: string | (() => string | null);

  close(): void;
  readyState?: number;
  onopen?: (() => void) | null;
  addEventListener(type: string, listener: (event: RuntimeStreamEvent) => void): void;
};

type EventSourceConstructor = new (url: string) => RuntimeEventSource;

function joinUrl(baseUrl: string, path: string) {
  if (!baseUrl) return path;
  return `${baseUrl.replace(/\/$/, "")}${path}`;
}

export function createWebRuntimeAPIs(options: WebRuntimeApiOptions = {}): RuntimeAPIs {
  const fetchImpl = options.fetchImpl

    baseUrl,

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:56:06.236Z

I'm considering whether `useCredential` could fail to hand out a bearer token for that profile, throwing a coded error, but since the runtime gets created at connect time and cached, `runtime.runner.restart()` ends up being a plain fetch call. I want to check how `useCredential` handles the reference and context more closely.

Rather than root-causing the exact error code, the defensible fix stands on its own: the restart request can't reliably report success since the server acknowledges before stopping anything and dies shortly after. Any client-observed failure is indistinguishable from that ordinary race, except an explicit refusal the server stated (`runner.restart.*`), which is the only case proving the runner is alive and didn't restart—so I should narrow the catch to surface only those codes. But I'm second-guessing whether that's right for something like a 401 session expiry, where the restart genuinely didn't happen and we'd risk silently closing out.

I'm reconsidering the rule: surface any HTTP status the client actually received (401/403/405/502/503) since those mean the server clearly answered and is alive, and swallow anything transport-level or opaque. But the current code already does this and still produced a false error, so the false positive must come from a coded path I haven't identified yet—and reproducing it directly would mean actually restarting the user's server, which isn't something I can do. I'm trying to think through what evidence I could gather without that, considering whether the 202 response with `redirect: "manual"` is really fine, or whether the runner dies before the response body finishes flushing.

I'm also tracing through `handleDialogAction`'s restart branch to confirm it's the code path that ran, and checking whether `getRuntimeAPIs()` returning null during a reconnect could silently no-op the restart call instead of throwing—concluding that's not the source of the error either. Now I want to check the git history to see if `isRuntimeTransportFailure` and its surrounding comment were added recently as a partial fix for this exact issue, and whether that fix actually made it into the running build.

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:56:36.881Z

I'm reconsidering the Aug fix that only addressed the transport-drop case—since the user's still hitting this, some coded error must actually be arriving now. Thinking through the runner setup, the process serves on 0.0.0.0:3050, so if Node kills it mid-request, the browser's pending POST would fail directly. But it's also possible the browser already got its 202 response successfully, and the error is happening somewhere else entirely.

I check the `close()` call on the UI manager, but that shouldn't throw. Then I realize something odd: `setBusy(true)` fires before the try block, and if the restart succeeds quickly, the dialog should close and the user shouldn't see an error at all—yet they're reporting one, meaning the catch block definitely ran somehow.

I consider whether the button click is firing twice, causing a second call to hit a dead connection and throw a swallowed TypeError, but that still doesn't quite explain what's visible to the user. I also check whether `getRuntimeAPIs()` could return null and short-circuit the restart call harmlessly. Now I want to look at how the credential store's `useCredential` hook behaves for same-origin requests.

I'm now considering whether `enforceSameOrigin` in `requireApiSession` is rejecting the request because the user is accessing the UI through an ngrok tunnel rather than localhost—there's a recent commit documenting ngrok setup, which makes this plausible. But the timing doesn't fit: the log shows the restart request succeeded and returned 202 at 00:51:44, meaning auth passed, so the same-origin check can't be the culprit here. The runner must have been killed almost immediately after responding successfully.

So I'm wondering if the client actually received that 202, or if something happened mid-response that made the fetch throw a coded error instead—maybe a redirect scenario where the fetch is configured with `redirect: "manual"` and the browser encounters an opaque redirect, which would throw `runtime.redirect_refused`. That could happen if the restart request somehow gets redirected to a newly-spawned runner process before the response completes.

Another possibility is that the browser retried the POST on a stale connection—browsers sometimes retry requests when a keep-alive socket closes before any response bytes arrive, even for POST in some implementations. If that retry hit the new runner while it was still booting, it could hit ECONNREFUSED (swallowed as a TypeError) or get some other unexpected response once the new runner came online.

A stronger candidate: there's a readiness gate that returns 503 while the runner is booting. If the retried POST landed during that window, the client would surface a generic `runtime.http_503` error even though the restart genuinely succeeded — which matches the user seeing an error while the restart worked fine. I should check the readiness gate for that 503 behavior.

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:57:04.896Z

I'm realizing the August fix only covered the "no reply" case, but there's a deeper issue: any reply synthesized during a process restart can't be reliably distinguished from a genuine success or failure by the client alone. The right fix isn't to guess based on the response received, but to actually verify the outcome afterward rather than trusting whatever the restart call returned.

Working through the specific error codes, I realize the only true failures are explicit refusals — the `runner.restart.*` codes and auth failures (401/403) — since these mean the server was alive and actively rejected the request. Everything else, including bare 502/503s without a body, network errors, aborts, or undefined responses, actually represents the process going away and coming back, which is exactly what a successful restart looks like. The key discriminator is that the restart route's own 502/503 responses always carry a `runner.restart.*` code in the body, so a bare `runtime.http_503` with no such code definitively did NOT come from the restart route itself — meaning I can safely swallow everything except codes that start with `runner.restart.`.

I need to verify whether `runtime.http_401` is actually reachable — checking if `requireApiSession`'s `jsonError(401, ...)` includes an error code or just message/source/action fields, since that determines whether `normalizeRuntimeHttpError` produces `runtime.http_401`. I'm settling on the cleanest framing: define a refusal as "a reply the restart route itself sent," and build a helper function that checks if an error's code starts with `runner.restart.` or matches one of the auth/method statuses (401/403/405). Then in the dialog's catch block, I only rethrow if `isRestartRefusal` is true — otherwise I treat it as the restart proceeding normally. I also want to improve the user-facing message for genuine refusals so it's more informative than a generic failure notice.

I'll add the restart-specific predicate directly in RunnerControls.tsx since that's where the domain knowledge lives. Let me also check whether there's an existing test file for RunnerControls to add coverage there.

describe("isRuntimeTransportFailure", () => {
  it("separates a server that answered from one that never replied", async () => {
    // Anything the server answered carries a code, whatever the status.
    const fetchImpl: typeof fetch = vi.fn(async () => new Response(
      JSON.stringify({ error: { code: "runner.restart.control_unavailable" } }),
      { status: 503 },
    ));
    const answered = await createFetchRuntimeRequest({ fetchImpl, surface: "web" })("POST", "/api/runner/restart")
      .then(() => null, (error: unknown) => error);
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure(answered)).toBe(false);

    // A status with no error body still gets a synthesised code.
    const bare: typeof fetch = vi.fn(async () => new Response("", { status: 502 }));
    const bareError = await createFetchRuntimeRequest({ fetchImpl: bare, surface: "web" })("POST", "/api/runner/restart")
      .then(() => null, (error: unknown) => error);
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure(bareError)).toBe(false);

    // The connection dying mid-request is what a restart actually looks like.
    const dropped: typeof fetch = vi.fn(async () => {
      throw new TypeError("Failed to fetch");
    });
    const droppedError = await createFetchRuntimeRequest({ fetchImpl: dropped, surface: "web" })("POST", "/api/runner/restart")
      .then(() => null, (error: unknown) => error);
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure(droppedError)).toBe(true);
  });

  it("treats missing and malformed rejections as transport failures", () => {
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure(undefined)).toBe(true);
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure(null)).toBe(true);
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure(new Error("boom"))).toBe(true);
    expect(isRuntimeTransportFailure({ code: 500 })).toBe(true);
  });
});

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-06T22:57:23.286Z

I'm noticing a bare 502 with no error body currently gets flagged as a failure, even though `normalizeRuntimeHttpError` already treats that exact case as "server unavailable" via `isBareServerUnavailable`. The restart dialog logic isn't consistent with that recognition, which is likely the exact scenario the user is hitting if something's proxying in front of the server.

I'm defining `RESTART_REFUSAL_CODES` to only include actual restart-route refusals (401/403/405) and writing an `isRestartRefusal` helper, since a restart can't self-report success — the process dies right after acknowledging, so anything else (dropped connections, bare 502/503, redirects) is just the normal shape of a restart succeeding.

Now I need to swap the restart dialog's catch block to only rethrow on genuine refusals, and remove the now-unused `isRuntimeTransportFailure` import since nothing else in the file references it. But before finalizing, I should verify whether `jsonError(401, ...)` actually attaches `runtime.http_401` as the code, since my refusal check depends on that exact shape — if the server formats it differently, my set won't catch it.

  "old_string": "function errorCode(error: unknown) {\n  if (error && typeof error === \"object\" && \"code\" in error) {\n    return String((error as { code: unknown }).code);\n  }\n  return \"runner.error.generic\";\n}",
  "new_string": "function errorCode(error: unknown) {\n  if (error && typeof error === \"object\" && \"code\" in error) {\n    return String((error as { code: unknown }).code);\n  }\n  return \"runner.error.generic\";\n}\n\n// Statuses the restart route itself answers with while the runner is still\n// alive and has stopped nothing. Its own refusals arrive under `runner.restart.*`\n// (see `handleRunnerRestartRequest`); these three come from the auth guard in\n// front of it, which rejects before the control service is ever called.\nconst RESTART_REFUSAL_CODES = new Set([\n  \"runtime.http_401\",\n  \"runtime.http_403\",\n  \"runtime.http_405\",\n]);\n\n/**\n * Did the runner refuse to restart, as opposed to going ahead and dying?\n *\n * A restart cannot report its own success. The control service acknowledges\n * before it stops anything, so the process answering this request is gone\n * moments later, and every shape that takes on the wire — a dropped connection,\n * a bare 502/503 from whatever still holds the port, a redirect into the\n * booting server's shell — is what success looks like from the browser. Only a\n * reply the restart route stated itself means nothing happened, and that is the\n * only one worth putting in front of the user.\n */\nfunction isRestartRefusal(error: unknown) {\n  const code = (error as { code?: unknown } | null | undefined)?.code;\n  if (typeof code !== \"string\") {\n    return false;\n  }\n  return code.startsWith(\"runner.restart.\") || RESTART_REFUSAL_CODES.has(code);\n}"
}

  "content": "import { describe, expect, it } from \"vitest\";\nimport { createFetchRuntimeRequest } from \"@/runtime-api/request\";\nimport { isRestartRefusal } from \"@/interface/runners/RunnerControls\";\n\nasync function rejectionFrom(fetchImpl: typeof fetch) {\n  return createFetchRuntimeRequest({ fetchImpl, surface: \"web\" })(\"POST\", \"/api/runner/restart\")\n    .then(() => null, (error: unknown) => error);\n}\n\ndescribe(\"isRestartRefusal\", () => {\n  it(\"surfaces a refusal the restart route stated itself\", async () => {\n    const refusal = await rejectionFrom(async () => new Response(\n      JSON.stringify({\n        error: {\n          code: \"runner.restart.control_unavailable\",\n          message: \"No restart control service is listening on port 3099.\",\n        },\n      }),\n      { status: 503 },\n    ));\n\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(refusal)).toBe(true);\n  });\n\n  it(\"surfaces an auth rejection that stopped the request before the control service\", async () => {\n    const rejected = await rejectionFrom(async () => new Response(\n      JSON.stringify({ error: { message: \"Authentication required.\" } }),\n      { status: 403 },\n    ));\n\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(rejected)).toBe(true);\n  });\n\n  it(\"treats a dying or returning server as a restart in progress\", async () => {\n    // The connection cut before any reply.\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(await rejectionFrom(async () => {\n      throw new TypeError(\"Failed to fetch\");\n    }))).toBe(false);\n\n    // A bare gateway status from whatever still answers on the port. The old\n    // rule surfaced this as \"The server operation failed\" on restarts that\n    // had in fact worked.\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(await rejectionFrom(async () => new Response(\"\", { status: 502 })))).toBe(false);\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(await rejectionFrom(async () => new Response(\"\", { status: 503 })))).toBe(false);\n\n    // The booting server redirecting into its app shell.\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(await rejectionFrom(async () => new Response(\"\", {\n      status: 302,\n      headers: { location: \"/\" },\n    })))).toBe(false);\n  });\n\n  it(\"treats missing and malformed rejections as a restart in progress\", () => {\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(undefined)).toBe(false);\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(null)).toBe(false);\n    expect(isRestartRefusal(new Error(\"boom\"))).toBe(false);\n    expect(isRestartRefusal({ code: 503 })).toBe(false);\n  });\n});\n"
}

## Claude · 2026-09-06T22:59:17.450Z

Fixed. What the dialog was doing wrong:

The restart path is deliberately fire-and-forget — the control service acknowledges *before* it stops anything (`restartCurrentWithEarlyAck`), because the process answering your request is the one about to die. An August fix already swallowed one shape of that race (`isRuntimeTransportFailure`: a rejection with no `code` at all, i.e. the connection cut before any reply). But that rule was too narrow. Anything that arrives as an actual HTTP status — a bare 502 or 503 from whatever still holds the port while the old process tears down and the new one boots, or a redirect into the booting server's app shell — gets a synthesised `runtime.http_*` code, which the dialog then read as "the server answered, so it refused" and showed `runner.error.generic`: "The server operation failed. Try again."

The rule is now the other way round: only a refusal the restart route *stated* counts as failure.

- `runner.restart.control_unavailable` / `control_unauthorized` / `control_failed` — the runner is alive, reached the control service, and nothing was stopped.
- `runtime.http_401` / `403` / `405` — the auth guard rejected before the control service was ever called.

Everything else is what a working restart looks like from the browser, so the dialog just closes and the registry reconnects.

`src/interface/runners/RunnerControls.tsx` holds the new `isRestartRefusal` predicate; `tests/interface/runners/restart-refusal.test.ts` covers both stated refusals and the four false-positive shapes (dropped connection, bare 502, bare 503, redirect). Suite is green, `tsc` clean apart from the pre-existing error in `tests/app/conversation-transcript-retention.test.ts`, eslint clean.

One thing I could not pin down: which exact status your browser received. The runner logged no error for `/api/runner/restart`, and `remote-restart.log` shows the 00:51:44 restart requested and completed normally — so the runner answered 202 and died moments later, and the browser saw the aftermath rather than a real refusal. Rather than guess at one status, the fix covers the whole class.

This ships in the interface bundle, so it needs a rebuild — the next restart runs `ensure-interface-build.mjs` and picks it up.
